II. 
Purpose and Overview of Testimony 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your direct testimony? 
17 
A. 
The purpose of our direct testimony is to present evidence and provide a 
18 
recommendation for the Company’s cost of equity (sometimes referred to as the 
19 


--- Page 6 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 4 of 112 
return on equity or “ROE”) and capital structure for rate-setting purposes for its 
1 
electric and gas distribution utility operations.  Our analysis and recommendations 
2 
are supported by the data presented in Exhibit __ (ROEP-1) through Exhibit __ 
3 
(ROEP-9) that were prepared or compiled under our supervision and direction. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please provide a brief overview of the analyses that led to your ROE 
6 
recommendation. 
7 
A. 
As discussed in more detail below, it is important to consider the results of several 
8 
analytical approaches in estimating the cost of equity.  To establish our ROE 
9 
recommendation, we developed a proxy group of companies that is a combination 
10 
of electric and gas utilities that face investment risk generally comparable to that of 
11 
Niagara Mohawk.  We developed a multi-stage Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) 
12 
model and two forms of the Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”).  We have 
13 
considered the range of results from each model and presented the DCF and CAPM 
14 
results weighted equally and with the Commission’s conventional two-thirds 
15 
weighting of the DCF and one-third weighting of the CAPM.   
16 
 
17 
The use of a multi-stage DCF model and two forms of the CAPM is consistent with 
18 
the approach employed by the Commission in prior cases.  As we explain in more 
19 
detail in our direct testimony, placing less emphasis on the DCF model at this time 
20 
is consistent with the goals of the Recommended Decision (“RD”) issued in the 
21 


--- Page 7 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 5 of 112 
Generic Finance Proceeding (“GFP”), Case 91-M-0509, which is the docket that 
1 
has been relied on by the Commission to establish the ROE formula.  
2 
 
3

Q. 
What are your conclusions regarding the appropriate cost of equity for 
1 
the Company? 
2 
A. 
Based on the quantitative and qualitative analyses discussed throughout our direct 
3 
testimony, based on the weighting of the DCF and CAPM results that has 
4 
historically been relied upon by the Commission and an equal weighting of the DCF 
5 
and CAPM results, and our assessment of the business and financial risks of 
6 
Niagara Mohawk relative to the proxy group, we conclude that the appropriate ROE 
7 
is within the range of 9.80 percent and 10.60 percent.  To mitigate the bill impacts 
8 
on customers, the Company is requesting an ROE of 9.50 percent, which is 
9 
somewhat below our recommended range of reasonableness, but is still within the 
10 
range of analytical results in Figure 1.  
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please summarize your analysis of the appropriate ratemaking capital 
13 
structure for the Company. 
14 
A. 
The Company’s requested equity ratio of 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent is at the 
15 
low end of the range of actual equity ratios for the companies in our proxy group 
16 
over the last four years.  Therefore, we conclude that the Company’s requested 
17 
equity ratio is reasonable, if not conservative, and suggests a higher level of 
18 
financial risk relative to the proxy companies.  As such, the incremental financial 
19 
risk would warrant a higher ROE than indicated by the mean results of the proxy 
20 
group analysis.  
21

III. 
Regulatory Guidelines and Financial Considerations 
Q. 
Please describe the guiding principles to be used in establishing the cost 
1 
of capital for a regulated utility. 
2 
A. 
The United States Supreme Court’s precedent-setting Hope and Bluefield cases 
3 
established the standards for determining the reasonableness of a utility’s allowed 
4 
ROE.  Among the standards established by the Court in those cases are:  (1) 
5 
consistency with the returns on equity investments in other businesses having 
6 
similar or comparable risks; (2) adequacy of the return to support credit quality and 
7 
access to capital; and (3) an understanding that the means of arriving at a fair return 
8 
are not controlling, only that the end result leads to just and reasonable rates.2 
9 
 
10 
Based on those standards, the Commission’s order in these cases should provide 
11 
the Company with the opportunity to earn a ROE that is:  (1) adequate to attract 
12 
capital at reasonable terms, thereby enabling it to continue to provide safe, reliable 
13 
service; (2) sufficient to support the financial soundness of the Company’s 
14 
operations; and (3) commensurate with returns on equity investments in enterprises 
15 
having comparable risks.  The authorized ROE should enable the Company to 
16 
                                                 
2  
Federal Power Commission v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591 (1944) (“Hope”); 
Bluefield Waterworks & Improvement Co., v. Public Service Commission of West Virginia, 
262 U.S. 679 (1923) (“Bluefield”). 


--- Page 11 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 9 of 112 
finance capital expenditures at reasonable rates and maintain its financial flexibility 
1 
over the period during which rates are expected to remain in effect. 
2 
Q. 
Has the Commission conducted a proceeding to review the standard for 
3 
estimating the cost of equity for a regulated utility? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  On August 21, 1991, the Commission issued an Order establishing a 
5 
proceeding commonly referred to as the GFP to review the Commission’s then-
6 
current methodology for estimating the cost of equity and to examine various 
7 
alternatives.3 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please provide a brief summary of the purpose of the GFP. 
10 
A. 
The GFP was initiated by the Commission on August 21, 1991, in its Instituting 
11 
Order because the Commission recognized that the DCF method was particularly 
12 
sensitive to interest rate fluctuations and was producing returns far below those of 
13 
other methodologies.4  The Commission’s goal in opening the GFP was to reduce 
14 
controversy around ROE calculations and to attempt to find common ground on 
15 
contentious issues by developing a consensus approach for setting utility equity 
16 
returns. 
17 
 
18 
                                                 
3  
Case 91-M-0509, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Consider Financial and 
Regulatory Policies for New York State Utilities, “Recommended Decision” (Issued July 
19, 1994), at 2 (“GFP RD”). 
4  
Id. 


--- Page 12 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 10 of 112 
The GFP examined whether there should be greater consistency in rate of return 
1 
determinations from company to company, such that differences in authorized 
2 
returns could be directly attributed to differences in risk between companies.  In 
3 
addition, the GFP examined whether the Commission’s historical primary reliance 
4 
on the DCF method continued to provide fair returns.5  The Commission’s inquiry 
5 
considered the merits of a generic process to reduce redundancy in litigating equity 
6 
returns and sought a robust, but standardized, approach to setting ROE such that 
7 
allowed returns would be commensurate with the risk of the individual company 
8 
and would not be skewed by the shortcomings of a single methodology. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Please describe the conclusions outlined in the RD of the Generic 
11 
Finance Proceeding. 
12 
A. 
Ultimately, the GFP RD concluded that the Commission should implement a 
13 
generic process for setting equity returns, based on proxy groups (not company-
14 
specific data), and that sole reliance on the DCF method should be replaced with a 
15 
combination of the DCF and CAPM methods.  The GFP RD proposed, as a 
16 
preferred convention, a respective two-thirds – one-third weighting of the results of 
17 
the DCF and CAPM analyses.  The GFP RD recognized that the CAPM “should 
18 
figure prominently in the analysis” because this methodology takes into account 
19 
                                                 
5  
Id., at 13-14. 


--- Page 13 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 11 of 112 
fundamental information on interest rates and the returns required by equity 
1 
investors as a result of changes in interest rates.  At that time, the CAPM was not 
2 
accorded the same level of prominence as the DCF analysis, given that the former 
3 
had previously been used only as a check.6  While the GFP RD recognized that 
4 
there was a benefit to establishing an “operating norm” with respect to weighting 
5 
the results of the DCF and CAPM methods in setting the ROE, it also recognized 
6 
that there may be good reason to either adjust the weightings of the DCF and CAPM 
7 
methods or to rely on different ROE estimation models.  Specifically, the GFP RD 
8 
provided the following guidance: 
9 
In either an annual-proceeding to determine a rate of return or 
10 
in individual proceedings, the 2/3 DCF and 1/3 CAPM 
11 
convention should be the presumption, but as Multiple 
12 
Intervenors suggests, parties would not be barred from 
13 
introducing new methods or different weightings.  Such parties, 
14 
however, would have the burden of convincing other parties 
15 
and the Commission of the relevance or superiority of their 
16 
proposals.7 
17 
 
18 
Although the GFP RD did not give equal weighting to the DCF and the CAPM,  
19 
there was an acknowledgement regarding the “varying degrees of acceptance in 
20 
utility rate cases.”8  Subsequently, as discussed in more detail in Section VI, in the 
21 
intervening decades since the GFC RD was issued, various regulatory commissions 
22 
                                                 
6  
Id., at 27. 
7  
Id. 
8  
Id., at 22. 


--- Page 14 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 12 of 112 
have relied on multiple models beyond the DCF in determining the cost of equity 
1 
in responses to changes in capital market conditions.  The GFC RD further notes: 
2 
There is no doubt that the CAPM should figure prominently in 
3 
the analysis, for it includes fundamental information on interest 
4 
rates and the relative returns needed by stocks in view of those 
5 
interest rates, and it provides information of what competitive 
6 
firms are earning.” 9  
7 
 
8 
To establish the “operating norm,” the GFP RD recommended specific forms of the 
9 
ROE estimation models – a two-stage DCF approach and a Traditional and Zero 
10 
Beta CAPM.  Although the GFP RD was never formally adopted by the 
11 
Commission, it has served as a touchstone for the Commission’s ROE 
12 
determinations for the past 20 years.  Given that various regulatory commissions 
13 
have demonstrated the application of multiple models in determining the cost of 
14 
equity in recent years, it is appropriate for the Commission to also consider multiple 
15 
models in the context of the current capital market conditions. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Does the analysis presented in the remainder of your direct testimony 
18 
meet the intentions of the GFP RD? 
19 
A. 
Yes, it does.  As discussed in greater detail in Section VI, the methodologies that 
20 
we have applied to estimate the return on equity are consistent with Commission 
21 
precedent since the RD in the GFP.  Moreover, the models used in our analysis 
22 
                                                 
9  
Id., at 27. 


--- Page 15 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 13 of 112 
extend the principles advanced in the RD in the GFP to best practices in financial 
1 
analysis and current capital market conditions, as was contemplated in the RD.       
2 
 
3 
Specifically, we consider the results of DCF and CAPM analyses.  In developing 
4 
these ROE estimation models, we rely on proxy groups of risk-comparable 
5 
companies as discussed in Section V.  We have used both the DCF and CAPM 
6 
methodologies to estimate the return on equity.  The multi-stage DCF model that 
7 
we relied on is consistent with the methodology that the Commission has relied on 
8 
in that it allows growth rates to vary over time.  Consistent with the fundamental 
9 
principles upheld by the Commission, we have applied two versions of the CAPM: 
10 
Traditional and Zero Beta.  Finally, consistent with the principles of the GFP, to 
11 
reduce the volatility associated with the reliance on any one model, we have 
12 
considered the range of each model, as well as the DCF and CAPM results weighted 
13 
equally, and with the RD’s proposed 2/3 weighting on the DCF and 1/3 weighting 
14 
of the CAPM. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Do the principles and intentions of the GFP RD require adherence to a 
17 
static formula? 
18 
A. 
No.  The GFP and RD did not require rote adherence to a static formula; rather, 
19 
they promoted some basic principles and afforded parties the flexibility to 
20 
investigate approaches to address changing financial market conditions.  The RD 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 14 of 112 
recognized the benefit of using multiple approaches for setting ROE and, although 
1 
it found benefits to a preferred convention for setting ROE, it did not bar parties 
2 
from introducing new cost of capital estimation methods or weightings and 
3 
specifically recognized that there may be circumstances where this would be 
4 
superior.  Capital market conditions vary widely over time and each ROE 
5 
methodology (DCF and CAPM) may be affected differently by identical 
6 
conditions.  The effect of these conditions on ROE must be assessed and interpreted 
7 
by the practitioner to determine if their effects are directionally appropriate and are 
8 
of a reasonable magnitude.  Accordingly, it is incumbent on the practitioner to 
9 
review the results of the analyses and exercise judgment as to how to weight those 
10 
results in the overall ROE determination.  A review of the RD reveals some 
11 
uncertainty around the correct level of weighting and certainly indicated a 
12 
willingness to revisit its proposed weightings in the future.  It is particularly fitting 
13 
that the Commission, which is seeking to update the traditional utility regulatory 
14 
model with new, innovative approaches suitable to current industry circumstances 
15 
in the New York Reforming the Energy Vision (“NY REV”) efforts, considers the 
16 
integrity of the intent and principles of the RD and demonstrate the flexibility to 
17 
adapt the weightings of each methodology to the applicable capital market 
18 
conditions.  Significantly, Moody’s expressed the view that NY REV “is at best 
19 
credit-neutral for utilities,” explaining that any rating impact from  NY REV “will 
20 
largely depend on the framework’s final characteristics and influence of three key 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 15 of 112 
credit drivers:  (1) the consistency and predictability of regulation, (2) the timeliness 
1 
of recovery of operating and capital costs; and (3) the sufficiency of rates and 
2 
returns.”10  Therefore, the rate of return becomes a key consideration as New York 
3 
looks to update its regulatory model. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Were there other methodologies that the parties considered throughout 
6 
the GFP and determined to be informative in establishing a fair and 
7 
reasonable ROE? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  In Case No. 91-M-0508, the parties, which included many of the electric and 
9 
gas utilities and the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) established a 
10 
Return on Equity Consensus Document (the “Consensus Document”) in June of 
11 
1993.  That document summarized the work that had been completed by the Electric 
12 
and Gas Industry Group (“EGIG”) to evaluate other methodologies to be used in 
13 
addition to or in substitution for the DCF methodology.11  The Consensus 
14 
Document recognized that the DCF model had produced volatile returns that were 
15 
highly sensitive to market conditions, in particular interest rates.  The Consensus 
16 
Document also recommended that the Commission rely on a Three Methodology 
17 
Approach to determine the allowed return on equity to be reflected in the rates of 
18 
                                                 
10  
Moody’s Investors Service, “New York's REV: Seeking a Greener Utility Grid for the 
Environment and Investors,” October 19, 2015, at 1, 4.  
11  
The EGIG was a group consisting of the nine major electric and natural gas utilities at the 
time, the Public Utility Law Project of New York, and Staff.  
 


--- Page 18 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 16 of 112 
the utilities including the DCF, the CAPM and a Comparable Earnings 
1 
methodology, which estimates the cost of equity using the earned and expected 
2 
returns of companies with similar risk.12  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Why is this important at this time?  
5 
A. 
 As will be discussed in Section IV, recent market conditions have resulted in 
6 
significant volatility that is not sufficiently addressed by the DCF and CAPM 
7 
models.  Therefore, it is important to consider at some level, the comparability of 
8 
the returns for other utilities of similar risk.  
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Has the Commission continued to consider modifications to its 
11 
regulatory approach as industry and market conditions have changed? 
12 
A. 
Yes, it has.  For example, in its ongoing “NY REV” docket, Case 14-M-0101, the 
13 
Commission has established a framework for updating the traditional utility 
14 
regulatory model with new, innovative approaches suitable to current industry 
15 
circumstances.  Likewise, it is equally appropriate for the Commission to consider 
16 
whether the methodologies and weightings used to estimate the cost of equity 
17 
continue to satisfy the intent and principles of the GFP RD under current capital 
18 
                                                 
12  
Case 91-M-0509, Return on Equity Consensus Document, June 2, 1993.  


--- Page 19 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 17 of 112 
market conditions and whether the returns produced by those methodologies and 
1 
weightings meet the requirements of Hope and Bluefield.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Is flexibility of approach and judgment important to an ROE 
4 
determination? 
5 
A. 
Yes, it is.  When faced with the task of estimating the cost of equity, analysts are 
6 
inclined to gather and evaluate as much relevant data (both quantitative and 
7 
qualitative) as can be reasonably analyzed.  Analysts and academics understand that 
8 
ROE models are tools to be used in the ROE estimation process and that strict 
9 
adherence to any single approach, or the specific results of any single approach, can 
10 
lead to flawed conclusions.  No model can exactly pinpoint the correct return on 
11 
equity; rather, each model brings its own perspective and set of inputs that inform 
12 
the estimate of ROE.  That position is consistent with the Hope finding that “[u]nder 
13 
the statutory standard of ‘just and reasonable,’ it is the result reached, not the 
14 
method employed, which is controlling.”13  
15 
 
16 
Though each model brings a different perspective and adds different information to 
17 
the analysis based on the underlying capital market conditions, each model also has 
18 
its own set of inherent weaknesses and should not be relied upon individually 
19 
                                                 
13  
 Hope, 320 U.S. at 602. 


--- Page 20 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 18 of 112 
without corroboration from other approaches.  Changes to inputs as a result of 
1 
changes in economic conditions could have widely different effects on the results 
2 
of the various analyses.   
3 
 
4 
Regardless of which analyses are performed to estimate the investor’s required 
5 
return on equity, the analyst must apply judgment to assess the reasonableness of 
6 
results and to determine the best weighting to apply to results under prevailing 
7 
capital market conditions.  No one model can reliably and consistently estimate the 
8 
cost of capital that meets the fairness standard of Hope and Bluefield in all market 
9 
conditions. 
10

12

14 
including recently authorized ROEs and expected returns for the proxy companies.  
15 
For example, since January 2019, authorized ROEs for electric and natural gas 
16 
utilities in fully litigated rate proceedings in other jurisdictions have been between 
17 
9.06 to 10.50 percent with a mean return of approximately 9.70 percent.14  The

1 
 
2 
IV. 
Capital Market Conditions 
Q. 
Why is it important to analyze capital market conditions? 
3 
A. 
The ROE estimation models rely on market data that are either specific to the proxy 
4 
group, in the case of the DCF model, or relate to overall expectations of market 
5 
return and risk, in the case of the CAPM.  The results of the ROE estimation models 
6 
can be affected by prevailing market conditions at the time the analysis is 
7 
performed.  While the ROE that is established in a rate proceeding is intended to 
8 
be forward-looking, the analyst uses current and projected market data, specifically 
9 
stock prices, dividends, growth rates and interest rates in the ROE estimation 
10 
models to estimate the required return for the subject company.  It is important to 
11 
consider whether the assumptions relied on in the current market or the projected 
12 
data are sustainable over the period that the recommended ROE would be in effect.  
13 
If investors do not expect current market conditions to be sustained in the future, or 
14 
the long-term assumptions embedded in the underlying analysis do not reflect long-
15 
term expectations, it is possible that the ROE estimation models will not provide 
16 
an accurate estimate of investors’ required return during that rate period. 
17 
 
18 


--- Page 22 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 20 of 112 
Q. 
What factors are affecting the cost of equity for regulated utilities in 
1 
the current and prospective capital markets? 
2 
A. 
The cost of equity for regulated utility companies is being affected by several 
3 
factors in the current and prospective capital markets, including: (1) elevated 
4 
market volatility widely attributed to the economic effects of Coronavirus disease 
5 
2019 (“COVID-19”), as well as other market factors, has created a short-term 
6 
aberration in the market which must be carefully considered when selecting the 
7 
inputs for the ROE estimation models; 2) utility stock valuations, which are 
8 
inversely related to dividend yields, are currently unsustainably high given 
9 
investors demand for defensive sectors during the short-term market dislocation; 
10 
and (3) recent Federal tax reform.  In this section, we discuss each of these factors 
11 
and how it affects the models used to estimate the cost of equity for regulated 
12 
utilities. 
13 
 
14 
    
A. 
Current Market Conditions 
Q. 
Please summarize current market conditions. 
15 
A. 
In 2020, market conditions have been extremely volatile.  In January and early 
16 
February 2020, major market indices were generally increasing, many reaching 
17 
new threshold levels.  By mid-February, as the global health pandemic became 
18 
more apparent, market conditions became increasingly more volatile.  In mid-
19 


--- Page 23 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 21 of 112 
February, just after utility stock prices reached an all-time high, the overall market, 
1 
including utility stocks, experienced a significant decline.  Market conditions in 
2 
March 2020 were even more volatile than the last half of February.  As shown in 
3 
Figure 2 below, the S&P 500 Index swung more than 3 percent in 16 of the 22 
4 
trading days in the month of March.  This level of volatility exceeds the volatility 
5 
that occurred in the 2008 Financial Crisis and prior to that period, had not occurred 
6 
for the previous 80 years.  As discussed in more detail later in our testimony, on 
7 
March 23, 2020, the Federal Reserve implemented unprecedented monetary policy 
8 
measures with the goal of providing liquidity and stabilizing market conditions.  In 
9 
the first half of this year, the Federal Reserve added nearly $3 trillion to its balance 
10 
sheet, a feat which took approximately five years following the Great Recession of 
11 
2008/2009.  As intended, the result of the Federal Reserve’s extensive intervention 
12 
has been that equity prices in April and May were less volatile than in March; 
13 
however, the fact that volatility remains elevated above historical average levels in 
14 
spite of the magnitude of these policies underscores the level of risk in the 
15 
marketplace. 
16

1 
Q. 
What steps have the Fed and Congress taken to stabilize financial 
2 
markets and support the economy?   
3 
A. 
On March 23, 2020, the Federal Reserve began a series of emergency programs to 
4 
support credit to large employers; the Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility 
5 
(“PMCCF”) to provide liquidity for new issuances of corporate bonds, and the 
6 
Secondary Market Corporate Credit Facility (“SMCCF”) to provide liquidity for 
7 
outstanding corporate debt issuances.  Further, the Federal Reserve supported the 
8 
flow of credit to consumers and businesses through the Term Asset-Backed 
9 
Securities Loan Facility (“TALF”).  Additionally, on March 27, 2020, the 
10 
Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security (“CARES”) Act was signed into 
11 
law, which is a large fiscal stimulus package aimed at mitigating the economic 
12 
effects of the coronavirus.  While these expansive programs have provided for 
13 
greater price stability, as shown in Figure 3, both the VIX and the TYVIX remained 
14 
well above long-term historical normal levels. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
How has the market responded to the unprecedented intervention by 
17 
the Federal Reserve? 
18 
A. 
The uncertainty surrounding the spread of COVID-19 resulted in a flight-to-quality 
19 
as investors purchased safer assets such as U.S. Treasuries due to increased fears 
20 
of a recession.  This has been increasingly evident over the past few months as 
21 


--- Page 29 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 27 of 112 
investors responded to news of the number of COVID-19 cases outside of China 
1 
and the economic effects of the policies enacted to contain COVID-19.  As 
2 
discussed above, however, in late March, the Federal Reserve began expansive 
3 
programs with the purpose of maintaining access to capital markets for corporate 
4 
borrowers.  These unprecedented programs resulted in lower borrowing costs for 
5 
corporate firms and supported access to the capital needed to offset the economic 
6 
effects of COVID-19.  As a result, interest rates have remained low and stability 
7 
has been restored in the corporate bond market.  For investors, this led to allocating 
8 
more funds to equities.  As shown in Figure 5, while the yield on the 10-year 
9 
Treasury Bond has remained relatively stable and in the range of 0.58 percent to 
10 
0.88 percent between March 23, 2020 and May 31, 2020, the S&P Utilities Index 
11 
increased drastically following the Federal Reserve’s announcement on March 23, 
12 
2020.  Therefore, the polices of the Federal Reserve, while resulting in stability in 
13 
the bond markets, have resulted in inflated equity prices as investors search for 
14 
returns given the current low interest rate environment. 
15

4 
effect on the cost of equity for Niagara Mohawk? 
5 
A. 
As discussed above, investors responded to the spread of COVID-19 by divesting 
6 
higher-risk assets and purchasing lower-risk assets such as U.S. Treasury bonds.  
7 
Furthermore, the constant news regarding the spread of COVID-19 and its 
8 
economic effects has resulted in an abundance of information for investors to 
9 
consider.  This has resulted in unprecedented volatility in financial markets as 
10 
investors have rotated in and out of various asset classes responding to both positive 
11 
and negative developments.  While the policies of the Federal Reserve have 
12 
stabilized the corporate bond market, the result has been an increase in equity prices 
13

2

3

8

9

10

11

12

B. 
The Effect of Market Conditions on Valuations 
Q. 
Please provide a brief summary of the recent monetary policy actions 
15 
of the Federal Reserve.  
16 
A. 
The Federal Reserve held a meeting on March 15, 2020 and acknowledged that the 
17 
recent spread of COVID-19 poses increased risks to economic activity in the U.S. 
18 
and therefore lowered the federal funds rate by 100 basis points, which resulted in 
19 


--- Page 32 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 30 of 112 
a range of 0.00 percent to 0.25 percent.17  This is the second unscheduled meeting 
1 
to occur in March, with the first being a March 3rd meeting where the Federal 
2 
Reserve decreased the federal funds rate by 50 basis points.  In addition to the 
3 
reduction in the federal funds rate, the Federal Reserve also announced plans to 
4 
increase its holdings of both Treasury and mortgage-backed securities.18  As 
5 
discussed previously, on March 23, 2020 the Federal Reserve also implemented an 
6 
expansive credit program designed to provide liquidity to corporations, large 
7 
employers, consumers, businesses and municipalities.  This program initially 
8 
targeted investment grade corporations, but in April 2020 was expanded to include 
9 
corporations that were investment grade rated as of March 22, 2020.  The PMCF 
10 
and SCCF programs were initially funded at $75 billion, but the combined size of 
11 
these programs, including the addition of below investment grade corporate debt is 
12 
proposed to be up to $750 billion.19  
13 
 
14 
It is important to view the recent Fed policy decisions in the context of the reactions 
15 
to global exogenous events, in particular COVID-19.  The recent spread of COVID-
16 
19 has affected the global economy and caused a rise in volatility in the financial 
17 
                                                 
17  
FOMC, Federal Reserve Press Release, March 15, 2020, at 1.   
18  
Id., at 2. 
19

--- Page 33 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 31 of 112 
markets; thus, the Federal Reserve reacted by reducing the federal funds rate to 
1 
minimize the effect of COVID-19 on the U.S. economy.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How has the Federal Reserve’s monetary policy affected capital 
4 
markets in recent years?   
5 
A. 
Extraordinary and persistent federal intervention in capital markets artificially 
6 
lowered government bond yields after the Great Recession of 2008-2009, as the 
7 
Federal Open Market Committee (“FOMC”) used monetary policy (both reductions 
8 
in short-term interest rates and purchases of Treasury bonds and mortgage-backed 
9 
securities) to stimulate the U.S. economy.  As a result of very low or zero returns 
10 
on short-term government bonds, yield-seeking investors have moved into longer-
11 
term instruments, bidding up prices and reducing yields on those investments.  As 
12 
investors have moved along the risk spectrum in search of higher yields that meet 
13 
their return requirements, there has been increased demand for dividend-paying 
14 
equities, such as natural gas and electric utility stocks.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
How have recent market conditions affected the valuations and 
17 
dividend yields of utility shares? 
18 
A. 
The Federal Reserve’s accommodative monetary policy has caused investors to 
19 
seek alternatives to the historically low interest rates available on Treasury bonds.  
20 
As a result of this search for higher yield, share prices for many common stocks, 
21 


--- Page 34 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 32 of 112 
especially dividend-paying stocks such as utilities, have been driven higher while 
1 
the dividend yields (which are computed by dividing the dividend payment by the 
2 
stock price) have decreased to levels well below the historical average.  As shown 
3 
in Figure 6, over the period from 2009 through February 18, 2020 (i.e., the peak of 
4 
the market prior to the recent decline resulting from the effects of COVID-19), 
5 
Treasury bond yields and utility dividend yields had declined.  While utility stock 
6 
prices, and the overall market, have declined due to the heightened risk aversion 
7 
and deteriorating economic conditions resulting from COVID-19, it is important to 
8 
recognize that utilities entered this period of turmoil at unusually high sector 
9 
valuation levels, in part because of Federal Reserve monetary policies that were in 
10 
place before the pandemic.  The high valuation of utility stocks was recently noted 
11 
by Charles Schwab:  
12 
[h]owever, amid the drop in stocks in February and March, the 
13 
historically low-equity-beta Utilities sector simply didn’t play 
14 
its traditional relative safe-haven role.  The sharp drop in 
15 
interest rates would normally be expected to provide relative 
16 
support to this sectors, which relies on high levels of debt and 
17 
tends to pay relatively high dividends—often an attraction for 
18 
investors when yields on fixed income investments are low. 
19 
However, there were unique circumstances that outweighed 
20 
these historical relationships.  
21 
 
22 


--- Page 35 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 33 of 112 
For one thing, because some investors had already been 
1 
reaching for yield before the crisis began, the high-dividend-
2 
paying Utilities sector had been bid up to record-high valuation 
3 
levels.  Even underperformance year-to-date hasn’t fully 
4 
reversed those relatively high valuations, so we’re not confident 
5 
the sector will return to its defensive roots if markets sell off 
6 
again.  
7 
 
8 
Additionally, with improving prospects for economic growth, 
9 
longer-term yields have begun to nudge higher, which further 
10 
reduces the sector's attractiveness.  The fundamentals of the 
11 
sector remain relatively strong, owing to still-low interest rates 
12 
supporting capital expenditure needs and stability of earnings 
13 
growth expectations.  However, the sector's short-term relative 
14 
performance has lagged amid a turn towards more cyclical 
15 
sectors.  Therefore, we are underweighting Utilities, despite 
16 
expected bouts of volatility in the overall market.20 
17 
 
18 
As discussed by Charles Schwab, the Federal Reserve’s increasingly aggressive 
19 
levels of monetary stimulus have only served to increase the pressure on utility 
20 
valuations.  As a result, current dividend yields should be viewed with caution when 
21 
used in DCF analysis to measure the forward-looking market required return 
22 
expectations of investors.  In this context, the more important indicator is the 
23

24

Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 36 of 112 
As noted by equity analysts, utility stocks have experienced high valuations and 
1 
low dividend yields, driven by investors moving into dividend paying stocks.  This 
2 
has occurred as a result of: 1) the low interest rates in the bond market; and 2) as 
3 
discussed above, the increased economic uncertainty in the market which has 
4 
resulted in equity investors rotating into defensive sectors such as utilities from 
5 
cyclical sectors, which are more likely to be affected by economic downturns.  
6 
Conversely, if economic conditions improve and interest rates increase, bonds 
7 
become a substitute for utility stocks and equity investors are more likely to rotate 
8 
back to cyclical sectors, which results in an increase in dividend yields.  As noted 
9 
previously, this change in market conditions that is expected over the long-term 
10 
implies that the ROE calculated using historical market data in the DCF model may 
11 
understate the forward-looking cost of equity.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What is the effect of high valuations on utility stocks on the DCF 
14 
model? 
15 
A. 
High valuations have the effect of depressing the dividend yields, which results in 
16 
overall lower estimates of the cost of equity from the DCF model. 
17 
Q. 
How do the valuations of public utilities compare to the historical 
18 
average? 
19 
A. 
Figure 7 summarizes the average historical and projected Price-to-Earnings (“P/E”) 
20 
ratios for the proxy companies calculated using data from Bloomberg Professional 
21 


--- Page 39 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 37 of 112 
and Value Line.24  As shown in Figure 7, the average P/E ratio for the proxy 
1 
companies increased from 2018 through early 2020 as a result of uncertainty in 
2 
markets surrounding the trade dispute between the U.S. and China and the spread 
3 
of the COVID-19.  The uncertainty has resulted in investors shifting to defensive 
4 
sectors such as utilities and consumer staples.  While P/E ratios for the proxy 
5 
companies have declined slightly in 2020, as investors have rotated from utilities 
6 
to Treasury Bonds due to the economic effects of COVID-19, the prices of utility 
7 
stocks and thus the P/E ratios are still at unsustainable levels.  For example, the 
8 
average P/E ratio for the proxy group from February 18, 2020 through June 16, 
9 
2020 (i.e., the period since the decline in the market as a result of COVID-19) was 
10 
19.75, which is well above the average for the period of 2000-2020 of 16.52.  It is 
11 
not reasonable to expect the proxy companies to maintain P/E ratios that are well 
12 
above long-term averages.  As shown in Figure 7, Value Line is projecting that P/E 
13 
ratios for the proxy companies will decline over the period of 2020 through 2025.  
14 
All else equal, if P/E ratios for the proxy companies decline, as Value Line projects, 
15 
the ROE results from the DCF model would be higher.  Therefore, the DCF model 
16 
using historical market data is likely understating the forward-looking cost of equity 
17 
for the proxy group companies. 
18

A. 
Yes.  To further assess how the current low interest rate environment has affected 
6 
the valuations of the companies in our proxy group, we reviewed the price/earnings 
7 
to growth (“PEG”) ratio for the S&P Utilities Index.  The PEG ratio is commonly 
8 
used by investors to determine if a company is considered over- or under-valued.  
9

--- Page 41 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 39 of 112 
The ratio compares the P/E ratio of a company to the expected growth rate of future 
1 
earnings.  This allows investors to compare companies with similar P/E ratios but 
2 
different earnings growth projections.  If two companies have a P/E ratio of 20.0, 
3 
but company A is growing at a rate of 6.00 percent and company B is growing at a 
4 
rate of 15.00 percent, then on a relative valuation basis company B is the better 
5 
investment.     
6 
 
 
7 
As shown in a report published by Yardeni Research, Inc., the PEG ratio for the 
8 
S&P Utilities Index is significantly higher than it has historically been because of 
9 
the accommodative monetary policy pursued by the Federal Reserve following the 
10 
Great Recession of 2008-2009.26  Although the PEG ratio has declined recently as 
11 
investors have rotated out of defensive sectors and into Treasury Bonds due to the 
12 
short-term economic effect of COVID-19, the PEG ratio for the S&P Utilities Index 
13 
is still above the historical average.  In general, stocks with lower long-term PEG 
14 
ratios are considered better values.  As the PEG ratio increases above the long-term 
15 
historical average, as has been the case with the S&P Utilities Index, then the stocks 
16 
are considered relatively over-valued unless the growth rate increases to support 
17 
the higher valuation.  As of May 29, 2020, the PEG ratio for the S&P Utilities Index 
18 
is close to 3.7, which indicates that many of the stocks in the index are currently 
19 
                                                 
26  
Yardeni Research, Inc. “S&P 500 Industry Briefing: Utilities,” June 4, 2020, at 5. 


--- Page 42 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 40 of 112 
trading at levels well above the historical average.  This analysis supports Value 
1 
Line’s expectation that the P/E ratios of utilities will decline over the near-term.

C. 
Effect of Tax Reform on the ROE and Capital Structure 
Q. 
Are there other factors that should be considered in determining the 
4 
cost of equity for Niagara Mohawk?  
5 
A. 
Yes.  The effect of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) should also be considered 
6 
in the determination of the cost of equity.  The credit rating agencies have 
7 
commented on the effect of the TCJA on regulated utilities.  In summary, the TCJA 
8 
is expected to reduce utility revenues because of the lower federal income taxes, 
9 
the end of bonus depreciation, and the requirement to return excess Accumulated 
10 
Deferred Income Taxes (“ADIT”).  This change in revenue is expected to reduce 
11 
Funds From Operations (“FFO”) metrics across the sector and, absent regulatory 
12 
mitigation strategies, is expected to lead to weaker credit metrics and negative 
13 
ratings actions for some utilities.27 
14 
 
15 
                                                 
27  
FitchRatings, Special Report, What Investors Want to Know, “Tax Reform Impact on the 
U.S. Utilities, Power & Gas Sector”, January 24, 2018. 


--- Page 43 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 41 of 112 
Q. 
Have credit or equity analysts commented on the effect of the TCJA on 
1 
utilities? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  From the outset of the TCJA, each of the credit rating agencies has indicated 
3 
that the TCJA would have an overall negative credit impact on regulated operating 
4 
companies of utilities and their holding companies because of the reduction in cash 
5 
flow that results from the change in the federal tax rate and the loss of bonus 
6 
depreciation. 
7 
 
8 
Moody’s noted that the rates that regulators allow utilities to charge customers are 
9 
based on a cost-plus model, with tax expense being one of the pass-through items.  
10 
In practice, utilities are collecting less income tax expense at a lower rate, reducing 
11 
revenue.  In addition, with the loss of bonus depreciation, the timing of future cash 
12 
tax payments has been accelerated.  Therefore, utilities are collecting less tax 
13 
revenue as a result of the lower tax rate and retaining less of the collected taxes as 
14 
a result of the loss of bonus depreciation.  All else being equal, the changes are 
15 
having a negative effect on utility cash flows and, ultimately, negatively impact the 
16 
utilities’ ability to fund ongoing operations and capital improvement programs. 
17 
 
 
18 
S&P observed that the utility industry’s financial measures weakened in 2018 and 
19 
attributed that to tax reform, capital spending, and negative load growth.  To 
20 
mitigate the anticipated weaker credit metrics, S&P expected that utilities to offset 
21 


--- Page 44 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 42 of 112 
the revenue reductions from tax reform with equity issuances.  Consistent with that 
1 
expectation, S&P reported that in 2018 regulated utilities issued nearly $35 billion 
2 
in equity, which is more than twice the equity issuances in either 2016 or 2017.28 
3 
 
 
4 
FitchRatings (“Fitch”) also indicated that any ratings actions will be guided by the 
5 
response of regulators and the management of the utilities.  Fitch notes that the 
6 
solution will depend on the ability of utility management to manage the cash flow 
7 
implications of the TCJA.  Fitch offered several solutions to provide rate stability 
8 
and to moderate changes to cash flow in the near term, including increasing the 
9 
authorized ROE and/or equity ratio.29 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Has Moody’s responded to the increased risk for utilities resulting from 
12 
the TCJA? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  Moody’s has been very active in considering the effects of the TCJA on the 
14 
industry.  In January 2018, Moody’s issued a report changing the rating outlook for 
15 
several regulated utilities from Stable to Negative.30  Moody’s noted that the rating 
16 
change affected companies with limited cushion in their ratings for deterioration in

Q. 
Is it reasonable to expect that investors have included the negative 
2 
effects of the TCJA on the cash flows of utilities in their valuation 
3 
models? 
4 
A. 
Not entirely.  It is reasonable to expect that investors have reviewed the reports 
5 
published by the credit rating agencies such as Moody’s, S&P, and Fitch and are 
6 
therefore considering the effects of the TCJA.  Utilities, however, are still managing 
7 
the negative effects of the TCJA and are working with regulators to determine 
8 
appropriate solutions to mitigate the effect of the TCJA on cash flows.  As Moody’s 
9 
noted in its November 2018 report, the TCJA is expected to continue to have a near-
10 
term effect on the cash flows of utilities, which resulted in Moody’s negative 
11 


--- Page 47 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 45 of 112 
outlook on the industry for 2019.31  Furthermore, as shown in Figure 8, Moody’s is 
1 
continuing to evaluate the effect of the TCJA on the cash flows of individual 
2 
utilities.  As part of the credit evaluation, rating agencies are specifically 
3 
considering the recent rate case decisions of utilities to determine if the results of 
4 
these cases help to mitigate the effect of the TCJA on cash flows.  Therefore, the 
5 
credit rating agencies appear to be continuing to monitor the effects of the TCJA 
6 
on utilities. 
7

to this.  Specifically, the Commission observed:

• The assumptions used in the ROE estimation models have been affected

2 
• Recent market conditions reflect short-term exogenous shocks that are not 
3

4

5 
when the rates for Niagara Mohawk will be in effect.

7

8 
assumptions to estimate the cost of equity that will be in effect over the 
9 
proposed rate period.  
10 
• Without adequate regulatory support, the TCJA will have a negative effect 
11 
on utility cash flows, which increases investor risk expectations for 
12 
utilities.

V. 
Proxy Group Selection 
Q. 
Please explain why you have used a group of proxy companies to 
15 
determine the cost of equity for the Company. 
16 
A. 
In these proceedings, we are focused on estimating the cost of equity for the 
17 
Company’s rate-regulated, electric and natural gas distribution utility operations in 
18 
New York.  Because ROE is a market-based concept and the Company is not 
19 


--- Page 50 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 48 of 112 
publicly traded, it is necessary to establish a group of companies that are both 
1 
publicly traded and comparable to the Company in certain fundamental business 
2 
and financial respects to serve as their “proxy” in the ROE determination process.  
3 
As discussed later in our direct testimony, the proxy companies used in our analyses 
4 
all possess a set of operating and risk characteristics that are substantially 
5 
comparable to the Company and thus provide a reasonable basis for the derivation 
6 
and assessment of the Company’s ROE. 
7 
 
8 
In utility rate proceedings before the Commission over the past 25 years (since the 
9 
RD in the GFP),33 the Commission has endorsed the use of proxy groups for the 
10 
purpose of determining utility ROEs.  Because proxy companies are now 
11 
commonly used as the basis for estimating the utility cost of equity, the primary 
12 
objective of the screening process is to establish a group of companies that are as 
13 
comparable as possible to the Company with respect to fundamental financial and 
14 
business risks.  As a practical matter, while the determination of an appropriate 
15 
ROE necessarily requires a degree of informed judgment, the careful selection of a 
16 
risk-appropriate comparison group serves to mitigate the extent to which subjective 
17 
assessments must be applied. 
18 
 
19 
                                                 
33 
Generic Finance RD at 133-134. 


--- Page 51 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 49 of 112 
Q. 
Please provide a summary profile of the Company. 
1 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk’s business consists of its regulated electric and natural gas 
2 
distribution operations in New York State.  Niagara Mohawk distributes electricity 
3 
to approximately 1.6 million retail customers and natural gas to approximately 
4 
600,000 retail customers in upstate New York.34  Niagara Mohawk’s long-term 
5 
issuer ratings are A3 from Moody’s and A- from S&P.35   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How did you select the companies in your proxy group? 
8 
A. 
In recognition of the Company’s combination electric and natural gas utility 
9 
operation, we began with the companies that Value Line classifies as “Electric 
10 
Utilities” and “Natural Gas Distribution Companies.”  That combined group 
11 
includes 47 domestic U.S. utilities.  We simultaneously applied the following 
12 
screening criteria to establish a risk-comparable proxy group that includes electric 
13 
and natural gas utility companies: 
14 
• To ensure that information regarding the proxy group companies is 
15 
consensus-based, we eliminated the companies that are not covered by at 
16 
least two utility industry equity analysts; 
17 
• We eliminated companies that do not have investment grade corporate 
18 
credit ratings and/or senior unsecured bond ratings according to S&P or 
19 
                                                 
34  
Source:  Company website. 
35 
Source:  SNL Financial, accessed May 29, 2020.  


--- Page 52 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 50 of 112 
Moody’s because such companies do not have a similar financial risk profile 
1 
to that of the Company; 
2 
• We eliminated companies that have not paid regular dividends or do not 
3 
have positive earnings growth projections from at least one source because 
4 
such characteristics are incompatible with the DCF model; 
5 
• To ensure that the proxy group consists of companies that are primarily 
6 
transmission and distribution regulated utilities, we eliminated companies 
7 
that have owned generation comprising greater than 60 percent of the 
8 
Company’s MWh sales to ultimate customers; 
9 
• To ensure that the proxy group consists of companies that are primarily 
10 
regulated utilities, we eliminated companies with less than 70 percent of 
11 
total operating income derived from regulated utility operations; 
12 
• We eliminated companies known to be party to a merger, acquisition, or 
13 
other transformational transaction as such activities may have a temporary 
14 
effect on such companies’ stock prices and projections unrelated to the 
15 
overall cost of capital; and 
16 
• We eliminated companies that do not have positive growth rates from at 
17 
least two sources. 
18

What is the composition of your proxy group?

A. 
Our proxy group consists of the companies in Figure 9.

2 
Q. 
Why do you believe that net operating income is an appropriate 
3 
screening criterion? 
4 
A. 
In establishing our proxy group, we relied on the percentage of net operating 
5 
income derived from regulated operations instead of the percentage of total revenue 
6 
derived from regulated operations because net operating income is more 
7 


--- Page 54 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 52 of 112 
representative of the contribution of that business segment to earnings and the 
1 
corporation’s overall financial position.  Specifically, a significant portion of gas 
2 
and electric utility company revenue is derived from the costs of purchased gas, 
3 
purchased fuel, and purchased power, which, in most cases, are recoverable through 
4 
tracking mechanisms and do not, therefore, contribute to earnings.  Furthermore, 
5 
this portion of total revenue can fluctuate considerably based on the cost of gas and 
6 
other inputs.  Therefore, relying exclusively on a revenue screen does not provide 
7 
a clear or necessarily consistent indicator of the contribution of the regulated utility 
8 
operations to a company’s earnings.  Net operating income excludes the cost of 
9 
purchased commodity and therefore more closely represents the contribution of the 
10 
business segment to earnings. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please provide an example of a company that has been included in the 
13 
proxy group because net operating income was used instead of total 
14 
revenue as a screening criterion. 
15 
A. 
South Jersey Industries, Inc. (“SJI”) would have been excluded from the proxy 
16 
group if the percentage of total revenue derived from regulated operations were 
17 
used as a screening criterion instead of the percentage of net operating income 
18 
derived from regulated operations.  SJI has an Energy Group segment that includes 
19 
unregulated, wholesale energy operations, retail natural gas and other operations 
20 


--- Page 55 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 53 of 112 
and retail electric operations.36  In 2019, the Energy Group segment had operating 
1 
revenues of approximately $688 million.37  When compared to SJI’s total operating 
2 
revenue of approximately $1.6 billion, it is clear that SJI’s percentage of revenue 
3 
derived from regulated operations would not meet the revenue screening criterion.38  
4 
Nonetheless, a substantial portion of Energy Group’s 2019 operating expenses are 
5 
related to natural gas and electricity purchases, therefore, profit margins are 
6 
dependent on commodity prices.  Thus, the Energy Group had operating income in 
7 
2019 of -$5 million.  As a result, the Energy Group segment does not represent a 
8 
large percentage of SJI’s net operating income.  As discussed above, net operating 
9 
income is the more appropriate screening criterion because it better approximates a 
10 
business segment’s contribution to earnings and the corporation’s overall financial 
11 
position.  For example, SJI operates a large natural gas distribution system in New 
12 
Jersey and is generally regarded as a gas distribution company.  The Energy Group 
13 
segment of NJR represents a large percentage of the company’s operating revenue 
14 
but represents a small percentage of net operating income.  SJI’s regulated 
15 
operations contribute a larger portion to the company’s earnings similar to Niagara 
16 
Mohawk and therefore should be included in the proxy group.  
17 
 
18 
                                                 
36  
South Jersey Industries, Inc., 2019 Form 10-K, at 102.  
37  
Id., at 103.  
38  
Ibid.  


--- Page 56 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 54 of 112 
Q. 
Do you believe that the 24 companies in your proxy group constitute a 
1 
sufficiently large proxy group? 
2 
A. 
Yes, we do.  The analyses performed in estimating the ROE are more likely to be 
3 
representative of the subject utility’s cost of equity to the extent that the chosen 
4 
proxy companies are fundamentally comparable to the subject utility.  Because all 
5 
analysts use some form of screening process to arrive at a proxy group, the group, 
6 
by definition, is not randomly drawn from a larger population.  Consequently, there 
7 
is no reason to place more reliance on the quantitative results of a larger and more 
8 
dissimilar proxy group simply by virtue of the resulting larger number of 
9 
observations. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Has the Commission typically relied on similar screening criteria when 
12 
estimating the ROE? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has typically relied on screening criteria that are similar to 
14 
those that we have used to develop our proxy groups.  The proxy group that is 
15 
typically relied on by the Commission is composed of a large group of dividend-
16 
paying companies with investment grade bond ratings and regulated revenues of at 
17 
least 70 percent that are not subject to merger-related or corporate restructuring 
18 


--- Page 57 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 55 of 112 
activities.39  For the reasons noted above and discussed throughout our direct 
1 
testimony, a proxy group based on these somewhat less selective criteria may be 
2 
less comparable to the Company than the proxy group we have relied on and 
3 
therefore may not produce appropriate estimates of the Company’s required ROE.  
4 
 
5 
VI. 
Cost of Equity Estimation 
Q. 
Please briefly discuss the ROE in the context of the regulated Rate of 
6 
Return. 
7 
A. 
The rate of return (“ROR”) for a regulated utility is based on its weighted average 
8 
cost of capital, in which the costs of the individual sources of capital are weighted 
9 
by their respective percentages of total capitalization of the utility.  The ROE 
10 
included in the ROR is weighted by the percentage of common equity in the 
11 
regulated utility’s ratemaking capital structure. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How is the required ROE determined? 
14 
A. 
While the cost of debt can be directly observed, the cost of equity and the required 
15 
ROE are market-based and, therefore, must be estimated based on observable 
16 
market information.  The required ROE is determined by using one or more 
17 
                                                 
39 
See, e.g., Case 13-E-0030, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. for 
Electric Service, Testimony of Craig E. Henry, at 14-16. 


--- Page 58 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 56 of 112 
analytical techniques that rely on market data to quantify investor expectations 
1 
regarding the range of required equity returns.  Informed judgment is applied, based 
2 
on the results of those analyses, to determine where within the range of results the 
3 
cost of equity for a company falls.  As a general proposition, the key consideration 
4 
in determining the cost of equity is to ensure that the methodologies employed 
5 
reasonably reflect investors’ views of the financial markets, the proxy group 
6 
companies, and the subject company’s risk profile. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What methods did you use to determine the Company’s cost of equity?  
9 
A. 
Consistent with Commission precedent, we used the DCF model and CAPM as the 
10 
primary approaches.  In establishing our recommended ROE, we relied on a multi-
11 
stage form of the DCF model, and, consistent with the Commission’s stated 
12 
preference, we used both the traditional form of the CAPM, as well as the Zero-
13 
Beta form of that model.  In both forms of the CAPM, we incorporated a forward-
14 
looking measure of the Market Risk Premium.  
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Why do you believe it is important to use more than one analytical 
17 
approach? 
18 
A. 
Because the cost of equity is not directly observable, it must be estimated based on 
19 
both quantitative and qualitative information.  When faced with the task of 
20 
estimating the cost of equity, analysts and investors are inclined to gather and 
21 


--- Page 59 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 57 of 112 
evaluate as much relevant data as reasonably can be analyzed.  As a result, a number 
1 
of models have been developed to estimate the cost of equity.  For that reason, we 
2 
use multiple approaches to estimate the cost of equity.  As a practical matter, 
3 
however, all of the models available for estimating the cost of equity are subject to 
4 
limiting assumptions or other methodological constraints.  Consequently, many 
5 
finance texts recommend using multiple approaches when estimating the cost of 
6 
equity.  For example, Copeland, Koller, and Murrin40 suggest using the CAPM and 
7 
Arbitrage Pricing Theory model, while Brigham and Gapenski41 recommend the 
8 
CAPM, DCF, and “bond yield plus risk premium” approaches.42  
9 
 
10 
Q. 
How are current market conditions affecting the results of the DCF and 
11 
CAPM models?  
12 
A. 
As discussed in Section IV, the results of the DCF model are understating the 
13 
forward-looking cost of equity.  The CAPM method offers some balance to the 
14 
sensitivity of the DCF model to low Treasury bond yields.  Low interest rates, 
15 
however, also impact the CAPM in two ways:  (1) if the risk-free rate is based on 
16 
                                                 
40 
Tom Copeland, Tim Koller and Jack Murrin, Valuation: Measuring and Managing the 
Value of Companies, 3rd Ed. (New York: McKinsey & Company, Inc., 2000), at 214. 
41 
Eugene Brigham, Louis Gapenski, Financial Management: Theory and Practice, 7th Ed. 
(Orlando: Dryden Press, 1994), at 341. 
42 
Although it has historically been our practice to present the results of a bond yield plus risk 
premium approach in the context of estimating a reasonable ROE, we have not done so in 
this case to limit the number of contested issues.  The result of such an analysis, however, 
would suggest more weight should be placed on the CAPM ROE determinations. 


--- Page 60 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 58 of 112 
historical average yields on Treasury bonds, it understates the forward-looking risk-
1 
free rate in the current capital market environment, and (2) if the market risk 
2 
premium is based on historical returns on large company stocks minus the historical 
3 
average risk free rate, it understates the forward-looking market risk premium.  To 
4 
adjust for these shortcomings, the risk-free rate in the CAPM analysis should also 
5 
consider projected yields on Treasury bonds, and the market risk premium should 
6 
be based on a forward-looking computation of the expected return on the total 
7 
market less the risk-free rate.  Market risk premiums based on long-term historical 
8 
averages are unresponsive to movements in interest rates and would likely 
9 
understate the market risk premium and, accordingly, the cost of equity in the 
10 
current capital market conditions.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What are your conclusions about the results of the DCF and CAPM 
13 
models?  
14 
A. 
The results of both models have been affected by market conditions and, with 
15 
traditional data inputs, have a tendency to underestimate the current cost of equity.  
16 
The DCF model is less reliable in current market conditions because dividend 
17 
yields for utilities are low and not expected to remain at current levels.  As discussed 
18 
in Section IV, reports from Value Line and Edward Jones demonstrate the expected 
19 
change in market conditions implies that the ROE calculated using historical market 
20 
data in the DCF model may understate the forward-looking cost of equity.  The 
21 


--- Page 61 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 59 of 112 
results from the CAPM are also affected by the current artificially low yields on 
1 
Treasury bonds.  The use of projected yields on Treasury bonds in the CAPM 
2 
produces returns that are more reflective of the market conditions that investors 
3 
expect during the period that the Company’s rates will be in effect.  Therefore, 
4 
properly specified, the CAPM may be a more reliable model in current market 
5 
conditions than the DCF.  Given the sensitivity of each model to market conditions 
6 
and considering the expectation for changes in those conditions, it is appropriate to 
7 
equally weight the results of the DCF and CAPM models. 
8 
 
9 
    
A. 
Discounted Cash Flow Model 
Q. 
Please describe the DCF approach. 
10 
A. 
The DCF approach is based on the theory that a stock’s current market price 
11 
represents the present value of all expected future cash flows.  In its most general 
12 
form, the DCF model is expressed as follows: 
13 
ܲ଴=
஽భ
ሺଵା௥ሻభ+
஽మ
ሺଵା௥ሻమ+ ⋯+
஽೙
ሺଵା௥ሻ೙ 
[1] 
14 
Where P0 represents the current market stock price, D1 … Dn are all expected future 
15 
dividends, and r is the discount rate, or required ROE.  As discussed below, we 
16 
have not included the constant growth form of the DCF model, but instead have 
17 
focused on a multi-stage form of the DCF model.   
18 
 
19 


--- Page 62 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 60 of 112 
Q. 
Please generally describe the DCF model you relied on.  
1 
A. 
The multi-stage DCF model is an extension of the constant growth form that 
2 
enables the analyst to specify growth rates over multiple stages.  As with the 
3 
constant growth form of the DCF model, the multi-stage form defines the cost of 
4 
equity as the discount rate that sets the current price equal to the discounted value 
5 
of future cash flows.  A multi-stage DCF model addresses the possibility that mean 
6 
five-year growth rates may not be reasonable in perpetuity and that payout ratios 
7 
could vary over time. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please describe the structure of the multi-stage DCF model.  
10 
A. 
The multi-stage DCF model that we have used sets each proxy company’s current 
11 
stock price equal to the present value of future cash flows received over three time 
12 
periods.  In all three periods, cash flows are equal to the annual dividend payments 
13 
that stockholders receive.  The first period is a short-term growth period that 
14 
consists of the first five years; the second period is a transition period from the 
15 
short-term growth rate to the long-term growth rate that occurs over five years (i.e., 
16 
years six through ten); and the third period is a long-term growth period that begins 
17 
in year eleven and continues in perpetuity.  The ROE is then calculated as the rate 
18 
of return that results from the initial stock investment and the dividend payments 
19 
over the analytical period. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 63 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 61 of 112 
Q. 
Has the Commission relied on a multi-stage DCF model in prior cases?  
1 
A. 
Yes, the Commission has relied on a two-stage form of the DCF model in prior 
2 
cases.43  The two-stage model that the Commission has relied on and the multi-
3 
stage model that we rely on both define the cost of equity as the discount rate that 
4 
sets the current stock price equal to the discounted value of future cash flows that 
5 
are expressed as projected dividends.  Both models project dividends using growth 
6 
rates over multiple periods.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Is the multi-stage form of the DCF model consistent with the intent of 
9 
the two-stage model relied upon by the Commission? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  Both the construction of the multi-stage model and the underlying 
11 
assumptions are consistent with the two-stage model relied upon by the 
12 
Commission.  The constant growth DCF model assumes the expected growth rate 
13 
will be constant in perpetuity.  The multi-stage forms of the DCF model, including 
14 
both the two-stage model that the Commission has relied upon and the multi-stage 
15 
form of the model that is relied on in our analysis, recognize short and long-term 
16 
growth prospects.   
17 
 
18 
                                                 
43 
See Case 10-E-0362, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, 
Rules and Regulations of Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. for Electric Service, “Order 
Establishing Rates for Electric Service” (Issued and Effective June 17, 2011) (“2011 O&R 
Rate Order”), at 68-69.   


--- Page 64 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 62 of 112 
Q. 
Does the multi-stage form of the DCF model offer improvements over 
1 
the two-stage model traditionally relied upon by the Commission? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The general form of the two-stage model relied upon by the Commission 
3 
involves a near-term growth stage based on projected dividends and a long-term 
4 
growth stage employing an estimated long-term growth rate in dividends.44  The 
5 
Commission’s application of a two-stage DCF assumes that a company’s growth 
6 
abruptly shifts to a long-run growth state after the initial five-year period.  In 
7 
contrast, the multi-stage model relies on growth rates over three periods, as 
8 
described above.  The multi-stage form of the DCF model provides for a gradual 
9 
transition to a company’s expected long-term growth, whereas the two-stage DCF 
10 
model assumes the transition from short to long-term growth occurs in one year.  
11 
 
12 
                                                 
44 
See, e.g., 2011 O&R Rate Order; Case 06-E-1433 et al., Proceeding on Motion of the 
Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of Orange and Rockland

1

3

4

5 
 
6 
Q. 
What growth rates did you rely on in the multi-stage DCF model? 
7 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (ROEP-1), we began with the current annualized dividend 
8 
as of May 29, 2020 for each proxy group company.  In the first stage of the model, 
9 
the current annualized dividend is escalated based on the average of the three-to 
10 
five-year earnings growth estimates reported by First Call, Zacks, and Value Line.  
11 
For the third stage of the model, we relied on long-term projected growth in Gross 
12 
Domestic Product (“GDP”).  The second stage growth rate is a transition from the 
13 
first stage growth rate to the long-term growth rate on a geometric average basis. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Why do you believe that earnings growth rates are the appropriate 
16 
growth rates to be relied on in the DCF model? 
17 
A. 
Earnings are the fundamental driver of a company’s ability to pay dividends; 
18 
therefore, earnings growth is the appropriate measure of a company’s long-term 
19 
growth.  In contrast, changes in a company’s dividend payments are based on 
20 
management decisions related to cash management and other factors.  For example, 
21 


--- Page 66 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 64 of 112 
a company may decide to retain certain earnings rather than include those earnings 
1 
in a dividend issuance.  Therefore, dividend growth rates are less likely than 
2 
earnings growth rates to reflect investor perceptions of a company’s growth 
3 
prospects.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Is there support for the use of analysts’ earnings growth estimates in 
6 
the DCF model? 
7 
A. 
Yes, there is significant academic support for the use of analysts’ earnings growth 
8 
rates.  In addition, the majority of the data that are publicly available to investors 
9 
sets forth analysts’ projections of earnings growth rates.  Value Line is the only 
10 
publication we are aware of that provides projected dividend growth rates. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Please summarize the academic research on growth rates and stock 
13 
valuation.  
14 
A. 
The relationship between various growth rates and stock valuation metrics has been 
15 
the subject of much academic research.  Many published articles specifically 
16 
support the use of analysts’ earnings growth projections in the DCF model in 
17 
general, as well as for a method of calculating the expected market risk premium in 
18 
particular.  While his report is focused on the calculation of the CAPM, Dr. Robert 
19 
Harris, demonstrates that financial analysts rely on earnings forecasts (referred to 
20 


--- Page 67 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 65 of 112 
in the article as “FAF”) and the use of a constant growth DCF formula to estimate 
1 
the expected market risk premium.45  Dr. Harris made the following observations:  
2 
[…] a growing body of knowledge shows that analysts’ 
3 
earnings forecasts are indeed reflected in stock prices.  Such 
4 
studies typically employ a consensus measure of FAF 
5 
calculated as a simple average of forecasts by individual 
6 
analysts.46   
7

Q. 
Do you believe that the Commission should revisit its historical reliance 
3 
on dividend per share growth rates during the initial five-year term of 
4 
its Two Stage DCF?  
5 
A. 
Yes, we do.  Sole reliance on Value Line projections of dividend per share growth 
6 
is not appropriate for several reasons.  First, the use of only dividend growth rates 
7 
ignores the substantial body of academic research demonstrating that earnings 
8 
growth rates are the most relevant factor in stock price valuation.55  Second, 
9 
projections of dividend growth, which would not include growth in retained 
10 
earnings, only measure a portion of a company’s growth.  Therefore, earnings 
11 
growth projections are more complete estimates of total company growth than 
12 
projected dividend growth rates.  Finally, Value Line’s four- to six-year projections 
13 
are not consensus estimates, but reflect the viewpoint of a single analyst.  
14 
Specifically, each stock covered by Value Line is assigned one specific analyst.  
15 
While there are various quality control procedures employed by Value Line’s 
16 
analysts, there is no process whereby multiple independent evaluations are 
17 
performed and then averaged together to form a consensus view.  Therefore, the 
18

Commission’s models, which have historically relied only on projected dividend

analyst in the first stage of the model.  In contrast, there are several consensus 
3

4 
and widely used by investors, including Zacks Investment Research and Thomson 
5 
First Call.  Each of these consensus forecasts considers the growth expectations for 
6 
each company based on the expectations of multiple analysts.  It is not reasonable 
7 
to exclude these timely and widely-available sources of information from the 
8 
analysis when it is likely that these real-time sources have become the more 
9 
common data points relied on by investors.

19

3 
 
4 
Q. 
Why is the long-term GDP growth rate a reasonable estimate of long-
5 
term growth in your multi-stage DCF model? 
6 
A. 
Long-term estimates of GDP growth are commonly used in regulatory proceedings 
7 
as a proxy for the long-term growth rate in the multi-stage DCF analysis.  That 
8 
application is based on the common theoretical assumption that, over the long-run, 
9 
all the companies in the economy will tend to grow at the same constant rate.  That 
10 
assumption is designed to address the uncertainty associated with estimating 
11 
individual company growth rates over very long time horizons and is not meant to 
12 
suggest that company growth rates in the economy will indeed converge in practice 
13 
over any given period.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Is your calculation of GDP growth consistent with analysts’ estimates 
16 
of long-term GDP growth? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  Investors understand that the U.S. economy goes through cycles of growth 
18 
and contraction.  Therefore, it is appropriate to consider the longest time period 
19 
                                                 
58 
U.S. Energy Information Administration, Annual Energy Outlook 2020, Table 20. 


--- Page 72 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 70 of 112 
possible to measure historical real growth in GDP.  This view is consistent with 
1 
Morningstar’s explanation about measuring GDP growth: 
2 
Growth in real GDP (with only a few exceptions) has been 
3 
reasonably stable over time; therefore, its historical 
4 
performance is a good estimate of expected long-term future 
5 
performance.  By combining the inflation estimate with the real 
6 
growth rate estimate, a long-term estimate of nominal growth is 
7 
formed.59 
8 
 
9 
Furthermore, Morningstar supports the use of long-term historical data:  
10 
The 87-year period starting with 1926 is representative of what 
11 
can happen: it includes high and low returns, volatile and quiet 
12 
markets, war and peace, inflation and deflation, and prosperity 
13 
and depression.  Restricting attention to a shorter historical 
14 
period underestimates the amount of change that could occur in 
15 
a long future period.  Finally, because historical event-types 
16 
(not specific events) tend to repeat themselves, long-run capital 
17 
market return studies can reveal a great deal about the future.  
18 
Investors probably expect “unusual” events to occur from time 
19 
to time, and their return expectations reflect this.60 
20 
 
 
21

How does your estimate of long-term GDP growth compare with 
22 
investor expectations of long-term utility industry growth rates?  
23 
A. 
The Commission has traditionally relied on Bank of America Merrill Lynch’s 
24 
(“BAML”) market return calculations in estimating a company’s ROE using the 
25 
CAPM.  Exhibit __ (ROEP-3) includes the relevant pages from the BAML

Yearbook, at 52. 
60

Quantitative Profiles reports for March 2020 through May 2020.  BAML derives 
1 
the Implied Return through the use of a multi-stage Dividend Discount Model 
2 
(“DDM”).  As shown in Exhibit __ (ROEP-3), the March, April, and May Implied 
3

4

5 
was 3.30 percent to 3.70 percent.62  Because the total return consists of capital 
6 
appreciation (i.e., growth) and dividend yield, that data suggest an expected utility 
7

8

9
