Q. 
How does your estimate of long-term growth differ from the estimate 
11 
the Commission has traditionally relied on? 
12 
A. 
The final stage of both the two-stage DCF model that the Commission has relied 
13 
on and our multi-stage DCF model extends into the future indefinitely.  Our long-
14 
term growth estimate reflects investors’ long-term growth expectations for the 
15 
period from 2031 through 2050.  Therefore, the third stage of our multi-stage DCF 
16 
model reflects investor growth expectations beginning in the first year of the third 
17 
stage of the model.  In contrast, the sustainable growth estimate for the two-stage 
18 
model that the Commission has typically relied on is based on short-term growth 
19 
                                                 
61 
Bank of America Merrill Lynch, Quantitative Profiles, March 2020, at 65; Bank of 
America Merrill Lynch, Quantitative Profiles, April 2020 at 15; and May 2020, at 62.  
62 
Id. 


--- Page 74 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 72 of 112 
rate forecasts.  The use of the sustainable growth rate, calculated using Value Line’s 
1 
published projections, provides an estimate of growth four to six years in the future.  
2 
As a result, the use of the sustainable growth rate in perpetuity in the second stage 
3 
of a two-stage DCF model does not provide a long-run estimate of growth.  Rather, 
4 
the use of the sustainable growth rate assumes that the short-term estimate for the 
5 
four- to six-year period from the Value Line report date is sustained in perpetuity.  
6 
 
7 
In contrast, the long-term growth rate in our DCF analyses reflects both economic 
8 
forecasts and market-derived projections of inflation over the longest available time 
9 
period (30 or more years).  Those estimates of long-term inflation expectations are 
10 
combined with the long-term average historical real GDP growth rate to calculate 
11 
an expected nominal GDP growth rate.  Consequently, the long-term growth 
12 
estimate in our multi-stage DCF model represents investors’ and economists’ views 
13 
of nominal long-term GDP growth well beyond the time horizon reflected in the 
14 
four- to six-year Value Line sustainable growth estimate relied on by the 
15 
Commission in prior cases.   
16 
 
17 


--- Page 75 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 73 of 112 
Q. 
Does the use of Value Line data to develop the Sustainable Growth 
1 
address concerns about growth rate bias? 
2 
A. 
No.  The sustainable growth rate is the sum of retention growth plus an SV factor,63 
3 
calculated using Value Line data.  As such, the sustainable growth rate estimate 
4 
that the Commission has relied upon is based on a single analyst’s viewpoint of a 
5 
company’s projected four- to six-year growth prospects.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Are there other problems with the use of the sustainable growth rate as 
8 
an estimate of long-term growth? 
9 
A. 
Yes.  Using the sustainable growth rate to estimate the long-term growth of the 
10 
company uses a very narrowly-defined set of short-term projections based on Value 
11 
Line data.  Specifically, it relies on the following assumptions: (1) projected 
12 
dividends for year two; (2) projected dividends for years four to six; (3) projected 
13 
earnings for years four to six; (4) projected book value for year two; (5) projected 
14 
book value for years four to six; (6) current estimate of actual outstanding shares of 
15 
stock; (7) projected shares of outstanding stock for years four to six; and (8) current 
16 
three-month stock price.  Each of these assumptions is estimated, at most, for six 
17 
years into the future.  As defined using these assumptions, the sustainable growth 
18 
                                                 
63 
Retention growth is the product of the expected earned ROE and the retention ratio (one 
minus the dividend payout ratio).  The SV factor employs an estimate of the market-to-
book ratio and the expected expansion rate of outstanding shares of common stock in the 
future.    


--- Page 76 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 74 of 112 
rate, which is applied over the long term in the Commission’s two-stage model, 
1 
does not consider any actual long-term forecasts for the specific company or the 
2 
economy.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding the methodology typically relied on 
5 
by the Commission to estimate the sustainable growth rate in the two-
6 
stage DCF model? 
7 
A. 
There are several reasons why the sustainable growth rate that the Commission has 
8 
used in the past should no longer be relied on in the two-stage DCF model.  First, 
9 
that “sustainable growth” rate is not a long-term measure of growth and, therefore, 
10 
should not be applied in perpetuity in the second stage of the model.  That is, the 
11 
sustainable growth rate methodology implicitly assumes that investors establish 
12 
long-term growth expectations based entirely on short-term, company-specific 
13 
projections.  In addition, the exclusive use of Value Line data, which is a single 
14 
analyst’s viewpoint, to establish the sustainable growth rate assumes that investors 
15 
do not consider any of the other financial information that is widely available when 
16 
establishing future dividend expectations.  It is unreasonable to conclude that 
17 
investors would ignore the expectations of long-term macroeconomic growth in 
18 
establishing the long-term growth estimates for an electric or natural gas 
19 
distribution utility or any other company.     
20 
 
21

Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 75 of 112 
Q. 
Have other regulatory commissions reconsidered the use of the 
1 
sustainable growth rates in the ROE estimation methodology? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  The Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s (“FERC”) long-standing 
3

4

5 
of earnings growth, as published by IBES; and (2) the sustainable growth rate, 
6 
calculated using the (b*r) + (s*v) components that are used by this Commission.  
7 
The FERC acknowledged that the sustainable growth rate is not a measure of long-
8 
term growth, but is another estimate of short-term growth similar to analysts’ 
9 
earnings projections.  
10 
 
11 
In Opinion No. 531, the FERC determined that it was appropriate to move from a 
12 
constant growth DCF methodology to a two-stage DCF model for public electric 
13 
utility rate cases.64  In moving to the two-stage DCF, FERC now relies on analysts’ 
14 
estimates of earnings growth in the short-term and a long-term GDP growth rate as 
15 
the measure of growth in the second stage.  The FERC’s application of the two-
16 
stage model precludes the need for a sustainable growth calculation.  This was 
17 
unchanged by the recently proposed methodology.  Although FERC has issued 
18 
several orders since Opinion No. 531 on the methodologies used to estimate the

B. 
Capital Asset Pricing Model  
Q. 
Please briefly describe the Capital Asset Pricing Model. 
4 
A. 
The CAPM is a risk premium approach that estimates the market cost of equity for 
5 
a given security as a function of a risk-free return plus a risk premium (to 
6 
compensate investors for the non-diversifiable or “systematic” risk of that security).  
7 
As shown in Equation [2], the CAPM is defined by four components: 
8 
ke = rf + β(rm – rf)  
 [2] 
9 
where:  
10 
 
ke = the required market ROE 
11 
 
β = Beta coefficient of an individual security 
12 
 
rf = the risk-free rate of return 
13 
 
rm = the required return on the market as a whole 
14 
 
15 
In this specification, the term (rm – rf) represents the market risk premium.  Based 
16 
on the theory underlying the CAPM, investors should be concerned only with 
17 
systematic or non-diversifiable risk because unsystematic risk can be diversified 
18 
away.  Non-diversifiable risk is measured by the Beta coefficient, which is defined 
19 


--- Page 80 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 78 of 112 
as: 
1 
β = 
  [3] 
2 
 
3 
The variance of the market return, noted in Equation [3], is a measure of the 
4 
uncertainty of the general market, and the covariance between the return on a 
5 
specific security and the market reflects the extent to which the return on that 
6 
security will respond to a given change in the market return. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What risk-free rate did you use in your CAPM analysis? 
9 
A. 
We used three estimates of the yield on Treasury bonds:  (1) the current three-month 
10 
average yield on 30-year Treasury bonds (1.37 percent);65 (2) the projected 30-year 
11 
Treasury yield for Q3 2020 - Q3 2021 (1.68 percent);66 and (3) the projected 30-
12 
year Treasury yield for the period 2022-2026 (3.00 percent).67  In determining the 
13 
security most relevant to the application of the CAPM, it is important to select the 
14 
term (or maturity) that best matches the life of the underlying investment.  As noted 
15 
by Morningstar: 
16 
                                                 
65 
Bloomberg Professional as of May 29, 2020. 
66 
Aspen Publishers, Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, Vol. 39, No. 6, June 1, 2020, at 2. 
67 
Id., at 14.

Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 79 of 112 
The traditional thinking regarding the time horizon of the 
1 
chosen Treasury security is that it should match the time 
2 
horizon of whatever is being valued…  Note that the horizon is 
3 
a function of the investment, not the investor.  If an investor 
4 
plans to hold stock in a company for only five years, the yield 
5 
on a five-year Treasury note would not be appropriate since the 
6 
company will continue to exist beyond those five years.68 
7 
Because utility companies represent long-duration investments, it is appropriate to 
8 
use yields on long-term Treasury bonds as the risk-free rate component of the 
9 
CAPM.  In our view, the 30-year Treasury bond is the appropriate security for that 
10 
purpose.  Because the cost of capital is intended to be forward-looking, it is 
11 
appropriate to consider projected measures of the market risk premium and interest 
12 
rates. 
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Does your use of the 30-year Treasury bond yield suggest that all 
15 
investors have an investment horizon of 30 years? 
16 
A. 
No, it does not.  As discussed above, the appropriate factor to consider in 
17 
determining what duration bond to use is the expected life of the assets.  As noted 
18 
by Morningstar, the use of the 30-year Treasury bond best matches the life of the 
19 
assets being valued, not the time horizon of the investor.  
20

Morningstar Inc., Ibbotson SBBI 2013 Valuation Yearbook, at 44.

Q. 
Please describe your estimate of the market risk premium used in your 
1 
CAPM. 
2 
A. 
The estimated market risk premium is based on the expected return on the S&P 500 
3 
Index, averaging estimates from both Bloomberg and Value Line, less the 30-year 
4 
Treasury bond yield.  The expected return on the S&P 500 Index is calculated using 
5 
a DCF model for all companies in the index based on market capitalization-
6 
weighted growth rates and dividend yields.  The market risk premium implied by 
7 
each of the three Treasury yields discussed above is used in the CAPM analysis 
8 
(see Exhibit __ (ROEP-5)). 
9

11

12 
 A.

13 
market risk premium, based on the difference between the estimated forward-
14 
looking required market return for the S&P 500, as provided by BAML, and the 
15 
risk-free rate.69  As a practical matter, that approach is consistent with the Market 
16 
DCF-derived forward-looking market risk premium estimate discussed above.

Q. 
What Beta coefficient did you use in your CAPM model? 
1 
A.

2

5

6 
 
7 
Q. 
Did you consider another form of the CAPM? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  In prior proceedings, the Commission has also relied upon the Zero-Beta 
9 
CAPM (the form of which is sometimes referred to as the “Empirical CAPM”70) in 
10 
estimating the cost of equity.  The Zero-Beta CAPM calculates the product of the 
11 
Beta coefficient and the market risk premium and applies a weight of 75 percent to 
12 
that result.  The model then applies a 25 percent weight to the market risk premium, 
13 
without any effect from the Beta coefficient.  The results of the two calculations are 
14 
summed, along with the risk-free rate, to produce the Zero-Beta CAPM result, as 
15 
noted in Equation [4] below:   
16 
 
ke = rf + 0.75β(rm – rf) + 0.25(rm – rf)  
[4] 
17 
where: 
18 
 
ke = the required market ROE 
19 
                                                 
70 
See, e.g., Roger A. Morin, New Regulatory Finance, Public Utilities Reports, Inc., 2006, 
at 189.   


--- Page 84 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 82 of 112 
 
β = Beta coefficient of an individual security 
1 
 
rf = the risk-free rate of return 
2 
 
rm = the required return on the market as a whole 
3 
In essence, the Zero-Beta form of the CAPM addresses the tendency of the 
4 
“traditional” CAPM to underestimate the cost of equity for companies with low 
5 
Beta coefficients such as regulated utilities.  The Zero-Beta CAPM is not redundant 
6 
to the use of adjusted Betas; rather, it recognizes the results of academic research 
7 
indicating that the risk-return relationship is different (in essence, flatter) than 
8 
estimated by the CAPM and that the CAPM underestimates the “alpha,” or the 
9 
constant return term.71   
10 
 
11 
As with the CAPM, our application of the Zero-Beta CAPM uses the forward-
12 
looking market risk premium estimates, the three yields on 30-year Treasury 
13 
securities noted earlier as the risk-free rate, and Bloomberg and Value Line Beta 
14 
coefficients.  Exhibit __ (ROEP-4) shows the CAPM results for the proxy group.  
15 
The traditional CAPM results range from 10.58 percent to 10.96 percent.  The Zero-
16 
Beta CAPM results range from 11.28 percent to 11.57 percent.  The range 
17 
established by an equal weighting of the traditional CAPM and the Zero-Beta 
18 
CAPM is 10.93 percent to 11.26 percent.72 
19 
                                                 
71 
Id., at 191. 
72  
The CAPM range is developed by averaging the traditional CAPM and Zero-Beta CAPM 
results for each of the three scenarios presented in Exhibit ___ (ROEP-4).

Q. 
Are the assumptions used in the CAPM less reliable than the 
11 
assumptions used in the DCF model? 
12 
A. 
Not necessarily.  As discussed previously, the CAPM relies on a risk-free rate, Beta 
13 
and the MRP.  The risk-free rate is readily observable and can be projected for the 
14 
forward-looking period.  Beta is estimated using the historical relationship between 
15 
the risk of the stock and the overall market.  Finally, the market risk premium, while 
16 
not observable, can be estimated for the forward-looking period.  Our testimony 
17 
discusses how the dividend yield has been affected by market conditions and 
18

therefore, although this assumption may be easy to calculate using historical data, 
1 
it is not representative of forward-looking market conditions.  Therefore, while the 
2 
CAPM is often criticized as relying on unobservable assumptions, currently the 
3 
dividend yield in the DCF model is not reflective of projected market conditions.

VII. 
Regulatory and Business Risks 
    
A. 
Risk Assessment 
Q.  
Are there recent developments that have increased the business risk to 
6 
gas distribution utilities generally in New York? 
7 
A.  
Yes.   New York passed the Climate Leadership and Community Protection Act 
8 
(“CLCPA”), which requires carbon free electricity by 2040 and net zero carbon 
9 
economy by 2050.  To comply with this law, residential and commercial customers 
10 
currently taking service from Niagara Mohawk will need to meet their energy needs 
11 
with carbon-free electricity or through low-carbon and renewable gas.  This could 
12 
result in significant financial and market risk for Niagara Mohawk resulting from 
13 
the uncertainty related to the cost recovery of the existing rate base investments as 
14 
well as ongoing maintenance of the existing system prior to the implementation of 
15 
the mandate.  Furthermore, Niagara Mohawk faces incremental financial risk from 
16 
a declining customer base, as customers migrate away from natural gas service over 
17 
time to comply with the mandate.  While the pathways to implementation of this 
18 


--- Page 89 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 87 of 112 
legislation have yet to be determined, the uncertainty surrounding this mandate 
1 
creates significant financial and market risk for utilities companies, especially 
2 
utilities with natural gas distribution operations. 
3 
 
4 
The full implications of this law on utilities has not yet been fully investigated or 
5 
understood but it creates uncertainties as it presents challenges as New York 
6 
considers alternatives to fossil fuel uses such as natural gas for heating and electric 
7 
power generation.  Moreover, on March 19, 2020, the Commission instituted Case 
8 
20-G-0131 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to Gas Planning 
9 
Procedures. The proceeding calls on utilities to present plans for incorporating non-
10 
pipeline solutions into their planning to avoid building new gas infrastructure and 
11 
reduce gas demand across their systems.  That means utilities would have to include 
12 
proposals for energy efficiency, electrification, and demand response programs in 
13 
their planning, and evaluate how those solutions could help reduce the need for 
14 
infrastructure investments.   Here, too, the uncertainty to the gas distribution 
15 
industry to meet the Commission’s goals will likely introduce more business and 
16 
regulatory risk to the gas distribution company model. 
17

B. 
Regulatory Environment 
Q. 
Please explain how the regulatory framework affects investors’ risk 
13 
assessments. 
14 
A. 
The ratemaking process is premised on the principle that, for investors and 
15 
companies to commit the capital needed to provide safe and reliable utility services, 
16 
the utility must have the opportunity to recover invested capital and the market-
17 
required return on such capital.  Regulatory commissions recognize that, because 
18 
utility operations are capital intensive, regulatory decisions should enable the utility 
19 


--- Page 96 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 94 of 112 
to attract capital at reasonable terms, thereby balancing the long-term interests of 
1 
investors and customers.  In that respect, the regulatory framework in which a utility 
2 
operates is one of the most important factors in both debt and equity investors’ risk 
3 
assessments.  
4 
 
5 
Because investors have many investment alternatives, even within a given market 
6 
sector, the Company’s authorized return must be adequate on a relative basis to 
7 
ensure its ability to attract capital under a variety of economic and financial market 
8 
conditions.  From the perspective of debt investors, the authorized return should 
9 
enable the Company to generate the cash flow needed to meet its near-term 
10 
financial obligations, make the capital investments needed to maintain and expand 
11 
its systems, and maintain sufficient levels of liquidity to fund unexpected events.  
12 
This financial liquidity must be derived not only from internally-generated funds, 
13 
but also from efficient access to capital markets.     
14 
 
15 
From the perspective of equity investors, the authorized return must be adequate to 
16 
provide a risk-comparable return on the equity portion of the Company’s capital 
17 
investments.  Because equity investors are the residual claimants on the Company’s 
18 
cash flows (i.e., debt interest must be paid prior to any equity dividends), equity 
19 
investors are particularly concerned with the regulatory framework in which a 
20 
utility operates and its effect on future earnings and cash flows. 
21 


--- Page 97 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 95 of 112 
 
1 
Q. 
Please explain how credit rating agencies consider the regulatory 
2 
framework in establishing a company’s credit rating.  
3 
A. 
Moody’s and S&P both consider the overall regulatory framework in establishing 
4 
credit ratings.  Moody’s establishes credit ratings based on four key factors:   
5 
Figure 12: Moody’s Rating Factors 
6

Weighting

25%

25%

40% 
 
Total 
100% 
 
7 
Two of these factors (i.e., regulatory framework and the ability to recover costs and 
8 
earn returns) are based on the regulatory environment such that half of Moody’s 
9 
overall assessment of business and financial risk for regulated utilities is based upon 
10 
the regulatory environment.82  Moody’s further subdivides the first two factors, 
11 
Regulatory Framework and the Ability to Recover Costs and Earn Returns, into 
12 
sub-factors to help “provide more granularity and transparency on the overall 
13 
regulatory environment, which is the most important consideration for this 
14 
                                                 
82 
Moody’s Investor Service, Rating Methodology, Regulated Electric and Gas Utilities, 
December 23, 2013, at 6. 
 


--- Page 98 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 96 of 112 
sector.”83 
1 
 
2 
With respect to Regulatory Framework, Moody’s looks for transparency, 
3 
predictability, and supportiveness of regulatory commissions.84  For the second 
4 
factor, Ability to Recover Costs and Earn Returns, Moody’s evaluates the 
5 
regulatory elements that directly affect the ability of the utility to generate cash 
6 
flow and service its debt over time.85  Moody’s views the ability to recover costs 
7 
on a timely basis and to attract debt and equity capital as crucial credit 
8 
considerations noting that “[t]he inability to recover costs…has been one of the 
9 
greatest drivers of financial stress in this sector.”86  This is particularly true as 
10 
utilities are often cash flow negative due to large capital expenditures, so any lack 
11 
of timely recovery or sufficiency of rates can strain access to capital markets. 
12 
 
13 
S&P has also identified the regulatory environment as an important factor, stating, 
14 
“we believe the fundamental regulatory environment in the jurisdictions in which a 
15 
utility operates often influence credit quality the most.”87 
16 
 
17

VIII. Capital Structure 
Q. 
Please summarize the Company’s proposed capital structure.  
1 
A. 
As explained in the direct testimony of the Company’s Capital Structure Panel, the 
2 
Company’s capital structure is based on a 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent equity 
3 
ratio for ratemaking purposes, which is more conservative than the Company’s 
4 
historical actual stand-alone equity ratio.  The requested equity ratio is consistent 
5 
with the Commission’s precedent regarding the appropriate equity ratio for 
6 
utilities.95  
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Have you conducted any analysis of the Company’s proposed capital 
9 
structure as compared with the proxy companies? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  We compared the 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent equity ratio to the authorized 
11 
capital structures of the companies in the proxy group for the period from 2016 
12 
through 2019.  As shown on Exhibit ___ (ROEP-9), the mean annual equity ratio 
13 
of the proxy companies over that period is 55.03 percent.  
14 
 
15 
                                                 
95  
See, e.g., Case 14-G-0319 et al., Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, 
Charges, Rules and Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Gas 
Service, “Order Approving Rate Plan” (Issued and Effective June 17, 2015); and Case 15-

Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 104 of 112 
Q. 
What do you conclude from this analysis? 
1 
A. 
The 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent equity ratio is conservative considering the 
2 
equity ratios of the proxy companies and the current business and financial risks of 
3 
Niagara Mohawk, including significant capital investment programs.  This analysis 
4 
shows that the utility operating subsidiaries owned by holding companies with 
5 
similar business characteristics to Niagara Mohawk have for the last four years 
6 
maintained average common equity ratios more than seven percentage points 
7 
higher than the 48.00 percent equity ratio, indicating that Niagara Mohawk will 
8 
have higher financial risk than the proxy group companies if it maintains its equity 
9 
ratio at 48.00 percent.  Therefore, we conclude that the requested equity ratio should 
10 
be considered a lower bound on the equity ratio that would support the Company’s 
11 
financial integrity and suggests a higher level of financial risk relative to the proxy 
12 
companies.  Accordingly, the incremental financial risk warrants a higher ROE than 
13 
indicated by the mean results of the proxy group analysis. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What is the Commission’s policy on determining the authorized equity 
16 
ratio?   
17 
A. 
The Commission has allowed the use of a stand-alone equity ratio if a utility can 
18 
demonstrate that the credit rating agencies view that utility’s credit on a stand-alone 
19 
basis independent of its parent. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 107 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 105 of 112 
Q. 
Do the credit rating agencies view Niagara Mohawk’s credit on a stand-
1 
alone basis?   
2 
A. 
Yes.  The credit rating agencies review and assess the credit risk profile of the 
3 
individual utility on a stand-alone basis, and Niagara Mohawk is rated on its own 
4 
financial merits and business risk profile.  The rating agencies point to the strong 
5 
ring-fencing provisions for the Company as a basis for its standalone evaluation. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how the Moody’s reports for Niagara Mohawk 
8 
demonstrate that Moody’s considers the Company’s credit quality on 
9 
a stand-alone basis. 
10 
A.   
In recent reports, Moody’s notes that Niagara Mohawk has strong ring-fencing 
11 
provisions that protect the stand-alone ratings. 
12 
While there is significant additional debt located at NiMo’s 
13 
parent holding companies, the strong ring-fencing provisions 
14 
applicable to NiMo reduce the potential for debt to be pushed 
15 
back down into NiMo, increasing its leverage.  In particular, the 
16 
explicit dividend payment restriction in case NiMo’s debt-to-
17 
capitalisation ratio exceeds 57% provides the greatest credit 
18 
support at the current rating level.  This provision compares 
19 
favourably against other utilities within the National Grid 
20 
group. 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 108 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 106 of 112 
Additional ring-fencing provisions imposed by the NYPSC for 
1 
NiMo that we view as credit supportive include: (1) a “special 
2 
preferred share” provision that reduces the probability of 
3 
bankruptcy in a distressed situation, and (2) the requirement for 
4 
NiMo to hold an investment grade rating.96 
5 
 
6 
Q.  
What do you conclude regarding the credit rating agencies’ view of the 
7 
credit quality of Niagara Mohawk? 
8 
A. 
Rating agencies are very cognizant of the protective ring-fencing measures that the 
9 
Commission has established for Niagara Mohawk and cite them as the reason why 
10 
they assess the Company’s credit quality on a stand-alone basis.  Because there is 
11 
factual evidence indicating that the two major credit rating agencies view the 
12 
Company’s credit quality on a stand-alone basis, the stand-alone capital structure 
13 
proposed in this proceeding is appropriate for the purpose of establishing the ROR 
14 
on rate base.   
15 
 
16 
Q.  
How will the capital structure and ROE authorized in this proceeding 
17 
affect the Company’s access to capital at reasonable rates? 
18 
A. 
The level of earnings authorized by the Commission directly affects the Company’s 
19 
ability to fund its operations with internally-generated funds.  Both bond investors 
20 
and rating agencies expect a significant portion of ongoing capital investments to 
21 
                                                 
96  
Moody’s Investors Service Credit Opinion: Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation, May 7, 
2019, at 5-6.   


--- Page 109 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 107 of 112 
be financed with internally-generated funds.  It also is important to realize that, 
1 
because a utility’s investment horizon is very long, investors require the assurance 
2 
of an authorized return that causes the coverage ratios to satisfy the long-run 
3 
financing requirements of debt holders.  Therefore, both the ROE and capital 
4 
structure are very important to debt and equity investors.  Furthermore, considering 
5 
the capital market conditions discussed in Section IV, the authorized ROE and 
6 
capital structure take on even greater significance.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Does the use of a 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent equity ratio have any 
9 
implications for your recommendation concerning the Company’s 
10 
ROE? 
11 
A. 
Yes.  The average equity ratio of the proxy companies is higher than 50.00 percent, 
12 
which means that, all else equal, the proxy companies have lower financial risk than 
13 
is implied by the 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent equity ratio proposed by the 
14 
Company.  Given this risk differential and the significance of the overall 
15 
ROE/capital structure determination to the Company’s continuing ability to access 
16 
capital, it is critically important that the Commission establish an ROE at least equal 
17 
to 9.50 percent. 
18 


--- Page 110 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 108 of 112 
IX. 
Multi-Year Rate Plan 
Q. 
Would a multi-year rate plan impact your ROE recommendation?   
1 
A.  
Yes, it would.  The Commission has in many cases approved three-year rate case 
2 
settlements that often include stay-out premiums.  It is our understanding that the 
3 
Company will provide two years of forecasted data in its rate filing in addition to 
4 
the forecasted rate year, for a total of three years of forecasted data.  In keeping 
5 
with Commission precedent, a stay-out premium would reflect the increased risk 
6 
faced by the Company under a multi-year rate plan.   
7 
 
8 
Q.  
How has New York typically estimated a stay-out premium?  
9 
A.  
The New York approach has typically set the measure of the risk and return trade-
10 
off using one half of the yield spread between a one-year and three-year Treasury 
11 
securities.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Does one half of the yield spread between one-year and three-year 
14 
Treasuries sufficiently reflect the risk to equity investors inherent in a 
15 
multi-year stay-out?  
16 
A. 
No.  The stay-out premium associated with a multi-year rate plan should not only 
17 
compensate investors for changes in the level of interest rates or inflation, but also 
18 
for the potential risk of under-earning that is introduced by “staying out.”  By 
19 
staying out of rate cases, the utility may not fully recover material amounts of 
20 


--- Page 111 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 109 of 112 
capital expenditures and may be required to absorb losses due to differences 
1 
between the cost of service established in the rate plan and actual levels of revenue 
2 
and expense.  The premium should compensate the utility and its investors for these 
3 
additional risks over and above interest rate risk.  In the current market 
4 
environment, there is additional risk that the authorized ROE for the latter years of 
5 
a multi-year rate plan will be lower than investors’ future requirements as interest 
6 
rates are expected to increase.  
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What do you propose as the stay-out premium for a three-year rate 
9 
plan?  
10 
A. 
The ROE proposed by the Company of 9.50 percent will not provide the Company 
11 
a return commensurate with the return available on investments of similar risk over 
12 
the term of the multi-year rate plan without an adequate stay-out premium.  
13 
Consistent with prior cases, we recommend that a stay-out premium be included in 
14 
the event a multi-year rate plan is adopted.  We believe 50 basis points is a 
15 
reasonable, albeit conservative, reflection of the incremental risk to the Company 
16 
under a multi-year stay-out provision.   
17 
 
18 


--- Page 112 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 110 of 112 
X. 
Conclusions and Recommendation 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding a fair return on equity for Niagara 
1 
Mohawk? 
2 
A. 
Our recommended return on book equity considers the results of the DCF and 
3 
CAPM models, summarized in Figure 13 (below), and the specific risks to which 
4 
the Company is exposed.  We conclude that the ROE range for the Company is 
5 
within the range of 9.80 percent and 10.60 percent based on the weighting of the 
6 
DCF and CAPM results that has historically been relied upon by the Commission 
7 
and an equal weighting of the DCF and CAPM.  To mitigate the bill impacts on 
8 
customers, the Company is requesting an ROE of 9.50 percent, which is somewhat 
9 
below our recommended range of reasonableness, but still within the range of 
10 
analytical results in Figure 13.  Furthermore, if the Commission approves the stay-
11 
out period, a premium of 50 basis points should be added to the ROE. 
12 


--- Page 113 ---
 
Testimony of the Return on Equity Panel 
Page 111 of 112

2 
Q. 
What is your conclusion regarding the Company’s proposed common 
3 
equity ratio? 
4 
A. 
Niagara Mohawk’s common equity ratio of 48.00 percent to 50.00 percent for 
5 
ratemaking purposes is consistent with the Commission’s equity ratio policy for 
6 
electric and natural gas distribution companies, but is below the mean and median 
7 
equity ratios for the operating companies held by the proxy group, and suggests a 
8 
higher level of financial risk relative to the proxy companies.  Further, the Company 
9 
has substantial ring-fencing provisions in place that insulate the operating utilities 
10 
from the parent company.  Therefore, relying on the ROEs determined for the proxy 
11 
companies understates the cost of equity for Niagara Mohawk and provides support 
12 
for a cost of equity higher than indicated by the mean results of the proxy group 
13 
analysis. 
14 
                                                 
97  
The DCF results presented in Figure 13 reflect the results of the models using low, average, 
and high growth rate assumptions.  The range of results for the CAPM is based on three 
interest rate scenarios, a historical average, a five-quarter projection, and a long-term 
projection.
